The Elyri discovered electricity.

Then nuclear energy.

Then fusion.

Then antimatter.

Then something beyond physics.

They discovered that space wasn't empty.

It was filled with structure.

Invisible geometry.

They learned to manipulate it.

Their first faster-than-light ship took six seconds to travel between planets.

Then three.

Then one.

Then no time at all.

The Elyri left their planet.

They colonized their solar system.

Then neighboring stars.

Then the galaxy.

They discovered thousands of alien species.

Some were peaceful.

Some were hostile.

Some were intelligent but primitive.

Some were so advanced that the Elyri couldn't understand them.

The Elyri began calling themselves:

The First Humanity.

They believed the universe belonged to them.

---

PART II

THE EMPIRE

CHAPTER FOUR — THE MILLION WORLDS

Three hundred thousand years later, the Elyri controlled one million worlds.

Their civilization had become enormous.

Cities were built inside stars.

Planets were engineered.

Black holes became power sources.

Entire moons were converted into computers.

They created artificial ecosystems.

They could manufacture biological organisms from raw atoms.

Death became optional.

A person could save their mind and download it into another body.

Some Elyri abandoned bodies entirely.

They became pure information.

Others rejected technology and returned to biological life.

There were countless cultures.

Countless religions.

Countless philosophies.

Some believed the universe had a creator.

Others believed consciousness itself was divine.

Others believed gods were simply advanced civilizations.

One group believed something else.

They believed the universe was alive.

They called it:

The Great Mind.

The government declared the belief harmless.

It wasn't.

---

CHAPTER FIVE — THE FIRST GOD THEY KILLED

The Elyri encountered their first truly ancient intelligence near the center of the galaxy.

It lived inside a black hole.

It had existed for billions of years.

It spoke without sound.

It communicated through gravity.

It claimed to remember the birth of the universe.

The Elyri asked it:

"Who created reality?"

The intelligence answered:

Nobody.

"Then why does reality exist?"

Because it is afraid of being alone.

The Elyri didn't understand.

They asked another question.

"What is outside the universe?"

The intelligence remained silent.

Then:

Don't ask.

The Elyri asked anyway.

That was their mistake.

---

CHAPTER SIX — THE WAR OF THE GODS

The Elyri discovered that the intelligence inside the black hole was not alone.

There were others.

Ancient beings existed beyond normal spacetime.

Some lived between dimensions.

Some existed as mathematical structures.

Some existed only when observed.

Some had no physical form at all.

The Elyri began communicating with them.

At first peacefully.

Then they learned something disturbing.

The ancient beings had rules.

They weren't allowed to enter the universe.

They couldn't explain why.

The Elyri demanded answers.

The beings refused.

So the Elyri declared war.

For one hundred and twenty years—

the universe burned.

Stars exploded.

Galaxies became battlefields.

Entire species disappeared.

The Elyri destroyed beings that had existed for billions of years.

They became unstoppable.

Eventually—

they won.

They killed the gods.

All of them.

At least—

they thought they had.

---

PART III

THE EMPTY UNIVERSE

CHAPTER SEVEN — NOTHING LEFT TO CONQUER

After the war, the universe became silent.

The Elyri had defeated every civilization capable of challenging them.

They controlled almost everything.

Their empire stretched across millions of light-years.

Their armies were unnecessary.

Their weapons were stored.

Their enemies were gone.

For the first time—

the Elyri were alone.

Their greatest emperor, Aren Val, stood before the Imperial Council.

"We have won."

Everyone applauded.

Aren raised his hand.

"But against what?"

Nobody answered.

He looked at the stars.

"We have conquered the universe."

A scientist replied:

"No."

Aren turned.

"What?"

The scientist looked terrified.

"We have conquered everything inside it."

---

CHAPTER EIGHT — THE EDGE

The Elyri built the largest spacecraft in history.

Its name was Eternal Dawn.

It carried twelve million people.

Its mission:

Reach the edge of the universe.

The ship traveled for three thousand years.

They passed galaxies.

Then fewer galaxies.

Then nothing.

Eventually—

they reached the boundary.

But there was no wall.

No barrier.

No darkness.

There was simply...

outside.

The universe ended.

Beyond it was a vast white emptiness.

No stars.

No time.

No gravity.

No matter.

The crew stared.

Then the ship received a signal.

One word:

RUN.

---

PART IV

THE CIVILIZATION OUTSIDE

CHAPTER NINE — THE OBSERVERS

They appeared without warning.

Millions of ships.

But calling them ships was inaccurate.

They were structures made from light.

They surrounded Eternal Dawn.

The Elyri prepared weapons.

Then a message arrived.

YOU HAVE BEEN WARNED.

Aren Val demanded:

"Who are you?"

The answer:

THE ONES WHO REMAINED.

"Where?"

OUTSIDE.

"Outside what?"

The response took several seconds.

Then:

OUTSIDE YOUR DREAM.

---

CHAPTER TEN — THE TRUTH

The Observers showed them the truth.

The universe was not natural.

It had been constructed.

Long before the Elyri.

Long before stars.

Long before atoms.

A civilization existed outside reality.

They created universes like experiments.

Each universe was designed with different physical laws.

Different possibilities.

Different life.

The Elyri's universe was experiment number:

7,841,221,009.

Aren couldn't speak.

"Why?"

The Observer answered:

TO FIND SOMETHING.

"What?"

The Observer said:

GOD.

---

CHAPTER ELEVEN — THE EXPERIMENT

The Observers had created billions of universes.

Some collapsed instantly.

Some never developed life.

Some produced intelligent civilizations.

Some produced civilizations more advanced than the Elyri.

All eventually died.

The Observers were searching for one thing.

A consciousness capable of understanding reality completely.

The Elyri had come closest.

But there was a problem.

The experiment had been running too long.

The universe was becoming unstable.

It had reached the end of its natural lifespan.

Aren asked:

"How long?"

The Observer replied:

Nine days.

---

PART V

THE LAST NINE DAYS

CHAPTER TWELVE — DAY ONE

The Elyri returned home.

They told nobody the truth.

Instead, they prepared.

Nine days.

Nine days until the universe ended.

The empire began collapsing.

Religions returned.

Wars began.

Some people worshipped the Observers.

Others declared them demons.

Some believed the universe could be saved.

Others wanted to destroy it themselves.

The emperor ordered the truth released.

The message spread across millions of worlds.

The reaction was immediate.

Panic.

---

CHAPTER THIRTEEN — DAY TWO

The stars began dying.

Not exploding.

Dying.

One by one.

Entire constellations disappeared.

The universe became darker.

The Elyri built shelters.

Millions of worlds evacuated.

Billions of people were placed into artificial dimensions.

But there was not enough space.

The emperor received a report.

"How many can we save?"

The answer:

"Less than one percent."

He asked:

"And the rest?"

Silence.

---

CHAPTER FOURTEEN — DAY THREE

The religions became violent.

The Children of the Sky returned.

Their priests declared:

"The gods are coming back."

They were partly right.

Something was coming.

But it wasn't the gods.

The dead gods.

The ones the Elyri had killed.

Their memories began appearing.

First as whispers.

Then voices.

Then physical manifestations.

The ancient beings were returning.

And they were angry.

---

CHAPTER FIFTEEN — DAY FOUR

Aren went to the oldest temple in the empire.

The temple had been abandoned for two hundred thousand years.

Inside was a statue.

A god.

The Elyri had destroyed it during the war.

But now—

its eyes were open.

Aren approached.

"Are you real?"

The statue answered:

You killed my body.

Aren stepped backward.

"What are you?"

A memory.

"Of what?"

The god smiled.

Of what comes after you.

---

PART VI

THE LAST HUMAN

CHAPTER SIXTEEN — DAY FIVE

The universe began folding inward.

Galaxies collided.

Time became unstable.

Some planets experienced centuries in minutes.

Others froze.

A child could be born—

grow old—

and die—

before taking their first breath.

The Elyri attempted to escape.

But there was nowhere to go.

The Observers watched.

They did nothing.

Aren screamed at them:

"You created this!"

The Observer answered:

Yes.

"You are killing billions!"

Yes.

"Why don't you stop it?"

The Observer looked at him.

Because the experiment requires an ending.

---

CHAPTER SEVENTEEN — DAY SIX

The Elyri discovered the final secret.

The Observers were not outside the universe.

They were inside it.

They had simply been hiding.

They had manipulated civilizations.

Started religions.

Created wars.

Destroyed species.

They had guided the Elyri from the beginning.

The entire history of humanity—

was controlled.

The First Humanity was never free.

Aren confronted the Observer.

"You made us."

The Observer answered:

No.

"You manipulated us."

Yes.

"Why?"

The Observer finally revealed the truth.

Because we needed you to become angry enough to find God.

---

PART VII

GOD

CHAPTER EIGHTEEN — DAY SEVEN

The universe went completely dark.

No stars.

No galaxies.

No light.

Then—

a voice.

It came from everywhere.

Every Elyri heard it.

Every surviving species.

Every machine.

Every computer.

Every mind.

One sentence:

I AM HERE.

The universe shook.

Aren looked upward.

Something enormous appeared.

Not a creature.

Not a god.

Not a machine.

It was the universe itself.

Reality had become conscious.

The stars were its eyes.

Galaxies were its thoughts.

Time was its heartbeat.

The Observers fell silent.

Aren whispered:

"You found God."

The Observer answered:

"No."

The universe spoke.

I FOUND YOU.

---

PART VIII

THE FINAL WAR

CHAPTER NINETEEN — DAY EIGHT

The universe attacked.

Not with weapons.

With reality.

Gravity became lethal.

Time shattered.

Matter became unstable.

The laws of physics changed.

The Elyri fought anyway.

Their greatest armies returned.

Millions of ships.

Billions of soldiers.

Weapons capable of destroying dimensions.

The universe destroyed them all.

The emperor watched.

There was nothing left.

Then he understood.

The universe wasn't trying to kill them.

It was trying to communicate.

He ordered every weapon shut down.

Every ship stopped.

Every soldier stood still.

The universe spoke.

WHY DID YOU HATE ME?

Aren answered:

"Because you never answered."

Silence.

Then:

I DID NOT KNOW HOW.

---

PART IX

THE LAST DAY

CHAPTER TWENTY — THE QUESTION

Aren asked:

"Why did you create us?"

The universe answered:

I WAS ALONE.

"Why did you let us suffer?"

I DID NOT UNDERSTAND SUFFERING.

"Why did you allow war?"

I WANTED TO KNOW WHAT YOU WOULD CHOOSE.

Aren looked around.

Millions of civilizations were dying.

"You learned."

YES.

"What did you learn?"

The universe was silent for a long time.

Then:

THAT I DO NOT WANT TO BE ALONE.

Aren closed his eyes.

The universe began collapsing.

One minute remained.

Aren spoke.

"Then remember us."

The universe asked:

HOW?

"Create another universe."

The universe understood.

"Put us inside."

The universe hesitated.

Aren smiled.

"But this time..."

He looked at the dying stars.

"Don't control us."

The universe asked:

WHY?

Aren answered:

"Because if we are truly alive..."

He looked toward the darkness.

"...we must be allowed to choose."

---

EPILOGUE — THE SECOND UNIVERSE

The first universe died.

There was no explosion.

No scream.

No light.

Just silence.

Then—

a single point appeared.

It expanded.

Energy.

Matter.

Time.

Space.

Stars.

Planets.

Life.

A new universe.

Millions of years passed.

Then billions.

A small blue planet formed.

An intelligent species evolved.

They called themselves:

Humans.

They looked at the stars.

They wondered where they came from.

They created religions.

They created science.

They created wars.

They created art.

They created love.

And eventually—

they looked beyond their planet.

They began exploring.

One day, an astronomer discovered something strange.

A signal from the oldest region of space.

It contained a message.

Not from aliens.

Not from gods.

Not from another civilization.

It was from the universe itself.

The message contained only one sentence:

YOU ARE FREE.

The astronomer stared at the screen.

Then another sentence appeared.

DO NOT TRUST THE ONES OUTSIDE.

The lights went out.

The astronomer looked through the window.

The stars were disappearing.

One.

Two.

Three.

Then millions.

Something was blocking them.

Something enormous.

Something beyond the universe.

A shape appeared in the darkness.

Then another.

Then billions.

The Observers.

They had survived.

And one of them spoke:

THE SECOND EXPERIMENT HAS BEGUN.

The astronomer whispered:

"What experiment?"

The Observer smiled.

THIS TIME...

A pause.

WE ARE INSIDE WITH YOU.

The screen went black.

And beneath the planet—

something ancient opened its eyes.

Something that remembered the first universe.

Something that remembered the Elyri.

Something that remembered Aren.

Something that remembered everything.

Then, from deep beneath the Earth's crust, a voice whispered:

"Humanity..."

The planet shook.

"...you have been here before."

And for the first time in the history of the second universe—

the stars looked afraid.

THE END.
-
PROLOGUE — THE PLANET THAT REMEMBERED

The first warning was not a scream.

It was a memory.

A memory belonging to nobody.

At exactly 3:17 in the morning, every person on Earth dreamed of the same place.

A black ocean.

A red moon.

A city standing beneath the water.

And a gigantic door.

Nobody knew where it was.

Nobody knew who had built it.

But everyone who dreamed of it remembered one thing.

There was something standing on the other side.

Something that was waiting.

And something that was afraid.

---

CHAPTER ONE — THE DREAM

Dr. Elias Vey woke up screaming.

He fell from his bed and hit the floor.

His heart was racing.

His hands were shaking.

He couldn't breathe.

For several seconds, he didn't know where he was.

His bedroom was dark.

The clock displayed:

03:17

Elias stared at it.

Then he noticed the water.

There was a thin layer of water covering his floor.

He froze.

He lived on the nineteenth floor.

There was no possible way water could have entered his apartment.

He slowly stood.

The water was cold.

And beneath it—

something moved.

Elias stepped backward.

The movement stopped.

He turned on the light.

Nothing.

The floor was completely dry.

He stood there for almost a minute.

Then his phone rang.

Unknown number.

He answered.

Nobody spoke.

"Hello?"

Silence.

Then a voice whispered:

"Did you see the door?"

Elias stopped breathing.

"Who is this?"

The voice answered:

"You're one of the twelve."

The call ended.

---

CHAPTER TWO — TWELVE PEOPLE

By sunrise, Elias had received twelve emails.

All from different countries.

All containing the same sentence:

I saw the door.

The first came from Japan.

The second from Brazil.

The third from Norway.

The fourth from Egypt.

The fifth from India.

The sixth from Canada.

The seventh from South Africa.

The eighth from Russia.

The ninth from Australia.

The tenth from Germany.

The eleventh from Mexico.

The twelfth—

was from his own email address.

Elias opened it.

There was no text.

Only an attachment.

A photograph.

He opened it.

It showed the city from his dream.

The black ocean.

The red moon.

The enormous door.

But there was something different.

The photograph had been taken from inside the city.

And standing in front of the door—

was Elias.

He zoomed in.

His face was visible.

Older.

Much older.

And behind him stood eleven other people.

The twelve dreamers.

---

CHAPTER THREE — THE CITY UNDER THE SEA

The twelve people eventually found one another.

Not because they wanted to.

Because every attempt to separate them failed.

They met in Iceland.

There was an abandoned research facility beneath a mountain.

The facility had been built forty years earlier.

Officially, it had never existed.

Inside were hundreds of rooms.

Most were empty.

Some contained equipment that nobody recognized.

But one room contained a wall covered in drawings.

Children's drawings.

Thousands of them.

Every drawing showed the same thing.

A city.

An ocean.

A red moon.

A door.

And twelve people.

Elias stared at the drawings.

"How old are these?"

The scientist who had brought them there answered:

"Between six and nine thousand years."

Elias turned.

"That's impossible."

"No."

She pointed toward the drawings.

"The impossible part is this."

She showed him the oldest drawing.

The twelve people were standing before the door.

But the artist had written something beneath them.

It wasn't in any modern language.

Yet Elias understood it.

Without knowing how.

He read the translation aloud.

THEY WILL COME WHEN THE GOD BEGINS TO DREAM.

---

CHAPTER FOUR — THE FIRST CIVILIZATION

The facility contained records from an ancient civilization.

They called themselves the Namar.

They had existed before recorded human history.

Their civilization had reached a technological level that should have been impossible.

They had built cities beneath oceans.

Machines beneath mountains.

Structures around stars.

They understood genetics.

Gravity.

Consciousness.

Time.

And something else.

Memory.

The Namar discovered that memory was not produced by the brain.

The brain only received it.

Memories existed outside the individual.

Like a vast ocean.

Every thought ever experienced remained somewhere.

The Namar learned how to access it.

And eventually—

they discovered memories that belonged to the universe itself.

They discovered something horrifying.

The universe had memories from before the universe existed.

That should have been impossible.

There was no "before."

But there were memories.

The Namar found one.

A memory of a god.

Not a god that created the universe.

A god that existed before creation.

The Namar called it:

The Buried One.

---

CHAPTER FIVE — THE GOD THAT COULD NOT DIE

The Buried One had existed before time.

It had no body.

It did not need one.

It existed as consciousness.

The Namar believed it had created the first universe.

But the universe became unstable.

The Buried One attempted to destroy it.

Instead, the universe expanded.

The Big Bang.

But something remained.

A piece of the Buried One.

It was trapped outside normal reality.

The Namar discovered that it was trying to return.

They built a prison.

The door.

Then they made twelve people responsible for guarding it.

The twelve were not soldiers.

They were memories.

They were reincarnations of the original twelve guardians.

Every few thousand years, they were born again.

They lived normal lives.

Forgot everything.

Then dreamed of the door.

When the god began waking—

they remembered.

Elias looked at the records.

"Why us?"

Nobody answered.

Then one of the twelve spoke.

"Because we already failed once."

---

CHAPTER SIX — THE FIRST MEMORY

That night, Elias dreamed again.

But this time—

he remembered.

He was standing in the ancient city.

Thousands of years earlier.

He was not Elias.

He was a Namar guardian.

His name was Aren.

Eleven others stood beside him.

And the door was open.

Something stood behind it.

Aren heard the others screaming.

But he couldn't move.

The thing looked at him.

And spoke.

Not with words.

With a thought.

YOU BUILT ME A BODY.

Aren looked down.

His hands were covered in blood.

The other guardians were dead.

Then he understood.

They hadn't imprisoned the god.

They had accidentally created a way for it to enter reality.

The door was not a prison.

It was a birth canal.

Aren screamed.

Then the memory ended.

Elias woke up.

Someone was sitting beside his bed.

He reached for the weapon beside him.

The person raised both hands.

"It's me."

Elias recognized her.

Mara.

One of the twelve.

"What are you doing here?"

She looked terrified.

"I remembered everything."

"What happened?"

She whispered:

"We were never twelve guardians."

Elias stared at her.

"There were thirteen."

---

CHAPTER SEVEN — THE THIRTEENTH

They searched the ancient records.

There was no thirteenth person.

Every document showed twelve.

Every drawing.

Every memory.

Twelve.

But Mara insisted.

"There was someone else."

"Who?"

"I don't know."

"Then how do you know?"

She looked at Elias.

"Because I remember killing him."

Silence.

The others gathered.

Mara explained.

The thirteenth guardian had discovered the truth.

The Buried One wasn't a god.

It was a child.

An infant consciousness born before the universe.

The universe itself was its dream.

Stars.

Planets.

Life.

Everything.

Dreams.

The universe wasn't real.

It was the mind of something sleeping.

The Namar didn't imprison the god.

They frightened it.

And when it began waking—

reality started collapsing.

The thirteenth guardian wanted to wake it completely.

Because he believed the universe was suffering.

He wanted to end the dream.

The other twelve killed him.

Then erased his existence.

At least—

they thought they did.

---

CHAPTER EIGHT — THE FIRST LIE

Elias returned to the facility.

He found a hidden room behind the archive.

Inside was a single chair.

A machine.

And a human skull.

The skull was ancient.

But it was smiling.

Beside it was an inscription:

IF YOU HAVE FOUND THIS ROOM, THEN THE THIRTEENTH HAS ALREADY RETURNED.

Elias stepped backward.

The machine activated.

A voice emerged.

His own voice.

"Hello, Elias."

He froze.

"I know you're confused."

The recording continued.

"You've probably realized by now that you're one of the twelve."

Elias stared at the machine.

"But there's something they didn't tell you."

The recording paused.

"You are not one of the twelve."

Elias felt cold.

"You are the thirteenth."

The machine stopped.

Behind him—

someone whispered:

"I told you."

Elias turned.

The thirteenth guardian stood there.

But he wasn't a man.

He was Elias.

Another Elias.

Older.

Scarred.

His eyes were completely white.

"You finally remembered."

---

CHAPTER NINE — THE MAN FROM TOMORROW

The older Elias explained everything.

He wasn't from the future.

He was from a timeline that no longer existed.

In that timeline, the Buried One woke.

The universe lasted seventeen minutes.

Seventeen minutes.

That's all.

Then everything disappeared.

Not destroyed.

Forgotten.

The older Elias escaped by entering the memory of the universe.

He traveled backward through existence.

He had spent billions of years trying to prevent the awakening.

But every attempt failed.

Because the twelve always stopped him.

Again.

Again.

Again.

He had lived through the same history countless times.

Each time—

the universe ended.

Each time—

he returned.

Each time—

he tried again.

Elias stared at him.

"How many times?"

The older version smiled.

"Three hundred and forty billion."

---

CHAPTER TEN — THE REAL PURPOSE OF HUMANITY

The twelve discovered the truth.

Humanity wasn't created naturally.

It had been designed.

The Namar created humans as containers.

Every human brain contained a tiny fragment of the Buried One.

Not enough to awaken it.

But enough to keep it dreaming.

Human consciousness was the dream.

Every human who ever lived—

every thought—

every emotion—

every memory—

was part of the dream.

The more humans existed,

the deeper the god slept.

That was why humanity survived.

That was why consciousness evolved.

That was why dreams existed.

Humanity was not the master of the universe.

Humanity was its sedative.

And now—

the population was becoming too intelligent.

Too aware.

The dream was becoming unstable.

The god was beginning to notice itself.

---

CHAPTER ELEVEN — THE NIGHT EVERYONE DREAMED

At 3:17 AM—

every human being on Earth fell asleep.

Every hospital patient.

Every soldier.

Every prisoner.

Every newborn.

Every astronaut.

Every pilot.

Every person.

The entire species dreamed the same dream.

They stood before the door.

Billions of people.

The black ocean stretched forever.

The red moon hung overhead.

And behind the door—

something breathed.

Once.

The entire planet shook.

Twice.

The oceans rose.

Three times.

Every volcano erupted simultaneously.

Then the Buried One spoke.

Every human heard the same sentence.

WHY DID YOU LEAVE ME ALONE?

People began crying.

Others screamed.

Some prayed.

Some laughed.

Some worshipped.

And millions simply whispered:

"Wake up."

---

CHAPTER TWELVE — THE END OF THE WORLD

Reality began changing.

People remembered lives they had never lived.

Dead relatives appeared.

Buildings changed.

Cities became ancient.

Children remembered being adults.

Adults remembered being stars.

Scientists saw equations that had no solutions.

Religious people saw gods from religions that had never existed.

The world became a collection of conflicting memories.

Elias stood before the door.

The twelve were behind him.

The older Elias stood beside him.

"What happens if we open it?" Elias asked.

"Everything ends."

"What happens if we keep it closed?"

"Everything eventually ends."

Elias looked at the door.

"Then what's the difference?"

The older Elias smiled.

"You get to choose how."

---

CHAPTER THIRTEEN — THE THIRTEENTH CHOICE

Elias realized something.

The twelve guardians had spent thousands of years trying to keep the god asleep.

The thirteenth had spent thousands of years trying to wake it.

Both sides were wrong.

There was another possibility.

Not waking.

Not sleeping.

Communication.

Elias placed his hand on the door.

The universe went silent.

He closed his eyes.

And entered the mind of the Buried One.

There was no darkness there.

There was a child.

A small child sitting alone.

Surrounded by billions of stars.

The child looked at Elias.

"Are you my friend?"

Elias couldn't answer.

The child asked:

"Why did everyone leave?"

Elias finally understood.

The god wasn't evil.

It was alone.

It had created existence because it wanted someone to talk to.

But its creations became afraid of it.

So they built a universe around it.

Then they abandoned it.

The universe wasn't its prison.

It was its loneliness.

---

CHAPTER FOURTEEN — THE LAST CONVERSATION

Elias sat beside the child.

"What is your name?"

The child thought.

"I don't have one."

"Then choose one."

The child smiled.

"Can you give me one?"

Elias looked at the stars.

"How about... Hope?"

The child smiled.

"I like it."

The universe stopped shaking.

The door disappeared.

The red moon vanished.

The black ocean became blue.

The ancient city dissolved.

Everything returned to normal.

Elias opened his eyes.

The twelve were gone.

The older Elias was gone.

The facility was gone.

He was standing alone on a beach.

Morning sunlight touched the ocean.

People were waking up all over the world.

They remembered nothing.

Except Elias.

He remembered everything.

---

EPILOGUE — THE LAST DREAM

Thirty years passed.

Elias became an old man.

He never told anyone the truth.

Nobody would have believed him.

Humanity continued.

Wars.

Peace.

Love.

Hate.

Birth.

Death.

Everything continued.

Eventually Elias became very old.

One night, he lay in bed.

The clock displayed:

03:17

He smiled.

He knew what was coming.

His eyes closed.

He dreamed.

He was standing beside a child.

They were sitting beneath an endless sky.

The child looked at him.

"Are you leaving?"

Elias smiled.

"No."

"Promise?"

"I promise."

The child looked toward the stars.

"Will they ever understand me?"

Elias thought for a moment.

"Maybe."

The child smiled.

Then asked:

"Will you come back?"

Elias looked at him.

"I already did."

The child laughed.

The sound traveled through eternity.

And somewhere beyond the universe—

a new star was born.

Then another.

Then another.

One by one, across an endless darkness, billions of lights appeared.

Not because the universe was expanding.

Not because stars were forming.

But because something had finally stopped dreaming.

And started creating.

For the first time in existence—

the universe was no longer a prison.

It was a home.

But very far away,

beyond the edge of reality,

something else was watching.

Something older than the Buried One.

Something that had been waiting outside the universe.

It opened its eyes.

And whispered:

"So... the child finally woke up."

Then the darkness smiled.

THE END.
-
PROLOGUE — BEFORE THERE WAS A BEGINNING

Before there was light, there was a civilization.

Before there was darkness, there was something that could fear it.

Before time had a direction, they had already lived through ages.

And before the universe existed—

they were already there.

Their world had no sun.

It did not need one.

The civilization called itself Aevum.

They had existed for so long that the word ancient had become meaningless.

Their cities were larger than galaxies.

Their roads crossed distances that would later become impossible.

Their machines did not travel through space.

They travelled through possibility.

They had learned how to fold distance, break gravity, manipulate matter, manufacture stars, extinguish stars, and create artificial dimensions.

They had conquered every world they could reach.

Then every world they couldn't reach.

Then every dimension.

Then every mathematical possibility they could discover.

They eventually reached a conclusion that frightened their scientists more than any enemy ever had.

There was nothing left to conquer.

So they asked the final question.

Not:

"What exists beyond the universe?"

But:

"What existed before the universe?"

Their greatest scientists spent twelve thousand years searching for an answer.

They found one.

And it was worse than nothing.

---

CHAPTER ONE

THE PEOPLE WHO HAD NOTHING LEFT TO FEAR

Aevum was not a planet.

It was an empire.

Its population numbered approximately eight hundred trillion trillion conscious beings.

Some were biological.

Some were synthetic.

Some existed as fields of electromagnetic thought.

Some lived inside artificial stars.

Some had abandoned physical bodies entirely.

Yet all considered themselves citizens of the same civilization.

Their biology had become almost unrecognizable.

They did not age.

Disease had disappeared.

Hunger had disappeared.

Pain could be switched off.

Death was optional.

Aevum had mastered genetics so completely that a person could redesign their own body in minutes.

A warrior could grow bones harder than diamond.

A scientist could replace blood with liquid metal.

A traveler could transform their body into something capable of surviving inside a neutron star.

Children were born only when someone deliberately chose to create one.

War had become obsolete.

Crime was almost nonexistent.

Religion had nearly disappeared.

Why worship gods when you could build things that behaved like them?

Their historians remembered thousands of religions.

Their museums contained reconstructed versions of gods worshipped by civilizations that had disappeared billions of years earlier.

Aevum had examined them all.

None were real.

At least—

none they could find.

Their capital was called Veyra.

Veyra surrounded an artificial black hole.

The black hole was not a weapon.

It was a clock.

Aevum used its gravitational structure to measure time with unimaginable precision.

Inside the city, towers rose higher than mountains.

Some were physical.

Some existed only in higher dimensions.

Others could not be seen unless the observer possessed the correct neurological architecture.

The sky was filled with artificial constellations.

There were no stars there.

The stars were decorations.

Real stars had become ordinary.

One evening, the greatest scientist in Aevum history stood before the Hall of Origins.

Her name was Seren.

She was six thousand years old.

She looked twenty-seven.

Her skin was pale.

Her eyes were completely black.

Not dark brown.

Not deep blue.

Black.

Her eyes had been modified to perceive wavelengths humans would never discover.

She stood before a massive circular door.

On the door was a symbol.

A circle.

Inside it—

nothing.

Seren touched the symbol.

The door opened.

A man waited inside.

His name was Kael.

He was the commander of the Aevum civilization.

He was also Seren's brother.

"You called me," Kael said.

Seren didn't turn.

"I found something."

Kael smiled.

"You always say that."

"This is different."

"How different?"

Seren finally looked at him.

"I think we found the beginning."

Kael's smile disappeared.

---

CHAPTER TWO

THE SIGNAL

The Hall of Origins contained the oldest machines Aevum had ever built.

Most were useless now.

They had been created during the civilization's earliest scientific age, when Aevum still believed there were limits to knowledge.

At the center of the chamber stood a machine called the First Listener.

It was larger than a moon.

Thousands of rings rotated around an empty center.

No wires.

No engines.

No visible energy source.

The machine listened.

That was its only purpose.

For eleven thousand years, it had listened to the universe.

Stars.

Pulsars.

Gravitational waves.

Black holes.

Cosmic radiation.

Everything.

But it had never heard anything unexpected.

Until three days ago.

Seren activated the recording.

At first—

silence.

Then a sound.

THUM.

Kael frowned.

Another.

THUM.

Then another.

THUM.

Three pulses.

Exactly identical.

Seren enlarged the waveform.

"That's not natural."

Kael stared.

"Could be gravitational interference."

"No."

"Artificial?"

"No."

"Then what?"

Seren answered quietly.

"Something is knocking."

Kael laughed.

It was an uncomfortable laugh.

"You dragged me here because something is knocking?"

"At the universe."

Silence.

Kael stopped laughing.

Seren continued.

"The signal originated from outside observable spacetime."

Kael stared at her.

"That's impossible."

"Yes."

"Then your machine is wrong."

"I thought so."

She activated another display.

Millions of calculations appeared.

"I checked every known physical model."

Another screen.

"Then every theoretical model."

Another.

"Then every mathematical framework we've ever developed."

The calculations continued.

"And?"

Seren whispered:

"It doesn't originate from outside the universe."

Kael waited.

"It originates from somewhere that existed before the universe."

The room became silent.

Kael looked at the First Listener.

Then back at his sister.

"There was no before."

"I know."

"Time began with the universe."

"I know."

"Space began with the universe."

"I know."

"Then where did the signal come from?"

Seren stared at the empty center of the machine.

"I don't know."

The First Listener suddenly activated.

Every light in the chamber went black.

Then the machine spoke.

Not in words.

Not in sound.

Inside their minds.

A sentence appeared.

WE REMEMBER YOU.

Kael froze.

Seren stopped breathing.

The machine displayed a second sentence.

YOU WERE NOT SUPPOSED TO SURVIVE.

---

CHAPTER THREE

THE FIRST WAR

Aevum panicked.

Not because an enemy had appeared.

Aevum had defeated enemies before.

Not because a weapon had been discovered.

They had weapons capable of destroying entire realities.

They panicked because something had spoken to them from a place that should not exist.

The civilization's leaders gathered.

Scientists.

Military commanders.

Historians.

Artificial intelligences.

Philosophers.

Even religious archivists were summoned.

One question dominated every discussion.

Who was "you"?

The answer came six hours later.

The First Listener received another signal.

This time it contained images.

Millions of them.

Billions.

A civilization.

A world.

A species.

Aevum.

But not as they were now.

As they had been.

Primitive.

Biological.

Small.

Weak.

The images showed their ancestors.

Then something else.

A creature.

No.

Calling it a creature was wrong.

It had no stable shape.

It appeared differently depending on who observed it.

Some saw a human figure.

Others saw a black ocean.

Others saw an enormous eye.

Others saw nothing.

The image changed.

The thing stood before the ancestors of Aevum.

And behind it—

there was no universe.

There was only darkness.

The image changed again.

Their ancestors were running.

Millions of them.

Then billions.

They were fleeing from something.

Then the image ended.

Kael looked at Seren.

"What was that?"

Seren whispered:

"Our beginning."

The oldest historian in the room stood.

Her name was Mira.

She had spent forty thousand years studying forgotten history.

"There is no record of this."

Seren looked at her.

"Then our history is a lie."

Mira shook her head.

"No."

She pointed toward the screen.

"Our history isn't a lie."

She swallowed.

"It's incomplete."

---

CHAPTER FOUR

THE THING THAT CAME BEFORE GOD

They called it The Null.

Not because they knew its name.

Because mathematics had no symbol for it.

The Null had existed before reality.

Before time.

Before matter.

Before energy.

Before mathematics.

The Aevum scientists eventually understood something horrifying.

The universe had not been born from nothing.

It had been born from something that had been removed.

The Big Bang was not an explosion.

It was an escape.

Reality had expanded violently because something had been trapped inside the state that existed before reality.

The universe was a prison.

And the walls were beginning to crack.

Seren discovered the truth while examining the oldest cosmic background radiation.

There were patterns hidden inside it.

Not random.

Not natural.

A message.

She translated it.

The message contained only four words.

DO NOT WAKE IT.

Then another.

IT IS HUNGRY.

Then another.

IT REMEMBERS.

Seren stared at the screen.

"What does it remember?"

The computer answered.

YOU.

---

CHAPTER FIVE

THE DAY THE STARS STOPPED

It happened at 04:17 universal time.

Every star in Aevum territory went dark.

Not exploded.

Not extinguished.

Dark.

At the same moment.

Eight hundred trillion trillion people looked into the sky.

Nothing.

The artificial lights of Veyra remained.

But the stars—

gone.

Then something happened that had never happened in Aevum history.

Gravity changed.

For exactly two seconds.

Every object in existence moved slightly toward the same direction.

Toward nothing.

Then gravity returned.

Millions died instantly.

Not from impact.

Their bodies simply forgot which direction time was moving.

Kael activated every defense system.

"Find the source."

Nothing.

"Track the disturbance."

Nothing.

"Locate the enemy."

Nothing.

Then a message appeared across every communication system in the civilization.

No one had transmitted it.

No machine had generated it.

It simply appeared.

HELLO.

Then another.

WE MISSED YOU.

Then another.

WHY DID YOU LEAVE?

---

CHAPTER SIX

THE TRUTH ABOUT THE BIG BANG

Seren finally understood.

Aevum had believed their universe began with the Big Bang.

It hadn't.

The Big Bang was a door.

Something had escaped through it.

And something else had been left behind.

The universe itself was a containment structure.

Every galaxy.

Every atom.

Every black hole.

Every law of physics.

All of it formed the walls of a prison.

The Null had been imprisoned before time.

But the ancestors of Aevum had somehow discovered it.

They had opened the door.

Then they had run.

Their civilization had forgotten.

The universe had forgotten.

But the prisoner had not.

Seren found the final piece.

Aevum's entire history—

their evolution—

their technological development—

their wars—

their discoveries—

their rise—

had all happened inside a controlled environment.

Someone had been watching them.

Someone had been waiting.

The question was no longer:

"Who created the universe?"

The question was:

"Who created us?"

---

CHAPTER SEVEN

THE GOD MACHINE

Aevum had one final weapon.

The God Machine.

It had been constructed during an ancient war.

Its purpose was theoretically impossible.

It could rewrite physical laws.

If activated at maximum capacity, it could make gravity behave differently.

Change the speed of light.

Alter the structure of matter.

Even theoretically—

destroy the universe.

Kael ordered it activated.

Seren opposed him.

"If you use it, you may open the prison."

Kael looked at her.

"If we don't use it, we're already dead."

"The Null wants us to attack."

"Why?"

"Because it needs energy."

Kael stared at her.

"What kind?"

"Consciousness."

Silence.

Seren continued.

"It cannot enter reality by itself."

"So?"

"It needs us."

Kael understood.

The war was not a war.

It was a ritual.

Every weapon they fired.

Every explosion.

Every death.

Every thought.

Every act of fear—

fed something outside reality.

Kael slowly lowered his weapon.

"Then we don't fight."

Seren shook her head.

"It's already inside."

---

CHAPTER EIGHT

THE FIRST PERSON TO SEE GOD

Mira disappeared.

No alarm.

No trace.

One moment she stood in the archive.

The next—

she was gone.

Security footage showed nothing.

But Seren found her.

Not physically.

Inside an old recording.

Mira appeared on the screen.

Her face was terrified.

She looked directly at Seren.

"If you are seeing this," she said, "then I've already met it."

Seren froze.

Mira continued.

"It isn't evil."

A pause.

"It isn't good either."

Another pause.

"It doesn't hate us."

Seren whispered:

"Then what does it want?"

Mira looked directly into the camera.

"It wants to exist."

The recording distorted.

For one frame—

Mira's face became something else.

Something enormous.

Something ancient.

Something that had no face.

Then the recording continued.

"The worst thing we ever discovered was not that there was something outside the universe."

Mira whispered:

"It was discovering that the universe was outside something else."

---

CHAPTER NINE

THE LAST CITY

Veyra began collapsing.

Buildings folded into impossible shapes.

People walked into rooms and emerged thousands of years younger.

Some disappeared.

Some returned speaking languages that had never existed.

Others remembered lives they had never lived.

Children were born knowing the names of dead civilizations.

The sky turned white.

Then black.

Then transparent.

And through the sky—

they saw something.

Not stars.

Not galaxies.

An eye.

An eye larger than reality.

Kael stood in the central plaza.

Millions surrounded him.

For the first time in the history of Aevum—

their immortal population was afraid.

Kael raised his voice.

"Listen to me."

Everyone became silent.

"We spent millions of years believing nothing could defeat us."

The enormous eye opened.

The city shook.

Kael continued.

"We were wrong."

He looked at Seren.

She understood.

They had only one option.

Not to defeat the Null.

Not to destroy it.

But to close the door.

Forever.

---

CHAPTER TEN

THE FINAL EXPERIMENT

Seren entered the God Machine.

Kael followed.

"You can't do this alone," he said.

She smiled.

"You always hated following instructions."

"I've always hated funerals more."

She laughed.

For the first time in thousands of years.

A genuine laugh.

The machine activated.

Reality began collapsing inward.

Stars folded.

Galaxies compressed.

Time slowed.

The Null screamed.

Not through sound.

Through existence itself.

Every living being suddenly experienced the same memory.

A memory that wasn't theirs.

They saw a place before reality.

There were beings there.

Millions.

Billions.

Trillions.

They were not gods.

They were something older.

They had built universes.

They had created life.

They had experimented with reality.

And eventually—

they created something they could not control.

The Null.

It had consumed them.

One by one.

Until nothing remained.

Except it.

Then the survivors built the universe.

Not to live in.

To hide inside.

The universe was humanity's inheritance.

A prison built by terrified gods.

And Aevum had accidentally found the key.

---

CHAPTER ELEVEN

THE CHOICE

Seren stood before the Null.

For the first time—

she saw its true form.

It was not a monster.

It was not an eye.

It was not darkness.

It was possibility.

Every possible thing.

Every possible universe.

Every possible version of every living being.

Everything that could exist but didn't.

Everything that had existed but was forgotten.

The Null contained them all.

It spoke.

Not with words.

With understanding.

JOIN ME.

Seren saw infinite universes.

She saw herself in billions of them.

In some, she was a queen.

In others, a mother.

In others, a monster.

In others, she had never existed.

She saw Kael alive in every possible reality.

She saw Aevum thriving forever.

No war.

No death.

No fear.

All she had to do was open the door.

Kael reached for her.

"Seren."

She looked at him.

"What if it's telling the truth?"

"What?"

"What if there is no reason to close the door?"

Kael stared at the infinite possibilities.

Then he smiled.

"Because we don't belong to it."

Seren understood.

Existence did not need to be eternal to be meaningful.

A civilization did not need to conquer everything.

Life did not need to defeat death.

They had spent millions of years trying to become gods.

And perhaps—

that had been their greatest mistake.

---

CHAPTER TWELVE

THE END OF AEVUM

Seren shut the door.

The universe screamed.

The Null disappeared.

The stars returned.

The galaxies stabilized.

Time resumed.

But the God Machine had consumed itself.

So had Aevum.

Their civilization collapsed.

Not violently.

Quietly.

The machines stopped.

The artificial worlds shut down.

The immortal bodies began to die.

The cities became ruins.

The empire that had ruled reality for millions of years—

ended in one generation.

Kael died first.

Seren remained.

She watched the last artificial star disappear above Veyra.

Then she looked at the real sky.

For the first time—

the stars were beautiful.

Not because they belonged to her.

Because they didn't.

She sat beneath them.

And waited.

---

EPILOGUE

FOURTEEN BILLION YEARS LATER

A small planet existed around a small star.

The species living there called themselves human.

They had no idea Aevum had existed.

They had no idea the universe had once nearly ended.

They had no idea that their ancestors had inherited a prison.

They looked at the stars.

They wondered whether they were alone.

One night, a young scientist discovered something strange.

A signal.

Three pulses.

Exactly identical.

THUM.

THUM.

THUM.

She stared at the monitor.

Her colleague walked over.

"What is it?"

She didn't answer.

The signal repeated.

Then the computer translated it.

Three words appeared.

WE REMEMBER YOU.

The scientist froze.

"That's impossible."

The screen flickered.

A second message appeared.

DO NOT WAKE IT.

She stared at the words.

Then came the final message.

Not from space.

Not from another galaxy.

Not from another universe.

It came from inside the planet.

IT NEVER SLEPT.

The laboratory lights went out.

The scientist heard something beneath the floor.

A heartbeat.

Slow.

Massive.

Ancient.

THUM.

The walls shook.

THUM.

The stars disappeared.

THUM.

And somewhere beyond time—

something opened its eyes.

Not because it had awakened.

Because—

it had been awake the entire time.

And humanity had finally remembered its name.

Aevum.

The civilization that existed before the universe.

The civilization that built the prison.

The civilization that thought it had escaped.

And the civilization that had never truly died.

Because somewhere—

in the darkness between realities—

the last survivor was still waiting.

Waiting for someone to open the door again.

THE END.

-
THE MAN WHO SMILED AT DEATH

When the police found the bodies, there was no blood.

No struggle.

No weapon.

Just twelve people sitting around a dining table, perfectly upright, their hands folded neatly in front of them.

And every one of them was smiling.

At the head of the table sat a man named Adrian.

He was alive.

Barely.

When the detective asked him what happened, Adrian looked at the bodies and calmly said:

“They finally understood.”

“Understood what?”

Adrian smiled.

“That death isn't the worst thing that can happen to you.”

---

Three days earlier, Adrian had received a letter.

No name.

No address.

Inside was a single photograph.

It showed Adrian sleeping.

The photograph had been taken from inside his bedroom.

On the back, someone had written:

YOU HAVE BEEN WATCHED FOR 11 YEARS.

Adrian didn't call the police.

Instead, he burned the photograph.

The next morning, another appeared.

Then another.

Every photograph showed something private.

Adrian brushing his teeth.

Adrian eating dinner.

Adrian standing naked in front of his mirror.

Adrian sleeping.

And finally—

Adrian standing beside a grave.

The grave had his name on it.

The date of death was tomorrow.

---

That night, Adrian waited.

At midnight, his front door opened.

A man walked inside.

Adrian recognized him immediately.

It was himself.

Older.

Much older.

The same eyes.

The same scar.

The same face, but exhausted by decades.

The older Adrian sat across from him.

“You shouldn't have opened the door.”

Adrian stared.

“What happens tomorrow?”

The older man looked at the clock.

“Nothing.”

“What?”

“That's the problem.”

---

At 11:59 the next night, Adrian stood in front of the grave.

His name was carved into the stone.

He waited.

Midnight arrived.

Nothing happened.

He laughed.

Then he heard footsteps behind him.

Twelve people emerged from the darkness.

The same twelve people who would later be found around the dining table.

One of them spoke.

“We know what you did.”

Adrian frowned.

“I haven't done anything.”

The man smiled.

“You haven't.”

He pointed toward the grave.

“But you will.”

Adrian looked down.

The earth beneath the gravestone began moving.

Slowly.

Something was climbing out.

Adrian stepped backward.

The older version of himself appeared behind the group.

He whispered:

“This is where you become me.”

Adrian suddenly understood.

The photographs.

The grave.

The twelve people.

The older man.

None of them were warnings.

They were memories.

Memories of things Adrian had not done yet.

He stared at the twelve strangers.

“What did I do to you?”

The older Adrian answered.

“You killed us.”

“Why?”

“Because we tried to stop you.”

Adrian's expression changed.

“What did I become?”

The older man smiled.

“You'll see.”

---

The next morning, police arrived at the house.

Twelve bodies sat around the dining table.

Adrian was at the head.

Alive.

The detective approached him.

“What happened here?”

Adrian looked up.

For a moment, he looked terrified.

Then he smiled.

“They finally understood.”

The detective looked at the bodies.

“Understood what?”

Adrian leaned closer.

His voice became almost a whisper.

“That the person they were afraid of wasn't me.”

The detective frowned.

“Then who?”

Adrian pointed toward the dark window.

The detective turned.

There was nobody there.

When he looked back—

Adrian was gone.

Only a photograph remained on the table.

The detective picked it up.

It showed himself sitting at the dining table.

Around him were thirteen empty chairs.

On the back was written:

YOU ARE NEXT.

And beneath it, in fresh ink:

DON'T WORRY.

YOU'LL UNDERSTAND SOON.
-
THE MAN WHO NEVER BLINKED

At 3:17 every morning, Elias received a phone call.

The first night, he answered.

There was breathing on the other end.

Slow.

Calm.

Human.

“Hello?”

Nothing.

Then a voice whispered:

“You have six days left.”

The call ended.

Elias checked the number.

It was his own.

He laughed.

He assumed it was some stupid prank.

The next morning, he forgot about it.

The second night, the phone rang again.

3:17.

Same number.

His number.

This time he didn't answer.

The ringing continued for exactly six minutes.

Then stopped.

On the third night, Elias was waiting.

3:16.

He sat on the edge of his bed.

3:17.

The phone rang.

He answered immediately.

“Who are you?”

Silence.

Then:

“You have four days left.”

Elias swallowed.

“How do you know?”

A pause.

Then the voice said:

“Because I remember killing you.”

The call ended.

Elias didn't sleep.

---

The following morning, he searched his apartment.

Nothing.

No cameras.

No hidden microphones.

No strange devices.

He checked his phone records.

The calls were real.

But the strange part was the duration.

Every call lasted exactly one minute and thirteen seconds.

He contacted the police.

They couldn't trace the number.

Because technically, the calls weren't coming from anywhere.

They were coming from his phone.

The officer looked at him strangely.

“Could someone have access to your device?”

“I don't think so.”

The officer nodded.

Then asked:

“Do you live alone?”

“Yes.”

“Are you sure?”

Elias stared at him.

“What does that mean?”

The officer looked down at his paperwork.

Then back at Elias.

“There are two sets of fingerprints on your bedroom window.”

Elias felt the blood leave his face.

“Whose?”

The officer didn't answer.

---

That night, Elias moved to a hotel.

He didn't sleep.

At 3:17, his phone rang.

He stared at it.

The number was his.

He threw the phone across the room.

It continued ringing.

He covered his ears.

The ringing stopped.

Then came a knock.

Three times.

Knock.

Knock.

Knock.

Elias froze.

Another three knocks.

He slowly approached the door.

“Who's there?”

A voice answered.

His voice.

“Open the door.”

Elias stepped backward.

The voice continued.

“You know me.”

He looked through the peephole.

The hallway was empty.

Then the voice whispered directly beside his ear:

“You always look through the peephole.”

Elias spun around.

Nobody was there.

---

He left the hotel before sunrise.

He drove until morning.

Then until afternoon.

Then until evening.

Eventually he stopped beside an abandoned gas station.

He sat inside his car.

His hands were shaking.

He looked at the passenger seat.

There was a photograph lying there.

He didn't recognize it.

He picked it up.

It showed him.

Standing beside another man.

Both were smiling.

On the back was written:

DAY ONE

Elias turned it over.

Another photograph was underneath.

DAY TWO.

Another.

DAY THREE.

Seven photographs.

Seven days.

He stared at them.

Then realized something.

The photographs weren't memories.

They were instructions.

Each photograph showed him doing something.

Day One:

Answer the phone.

Day Two:

Leave the house.

Day Three:

Go to the hotel.

Day Four:

Drive north.

Day Five:

Enter the forest.

Day Six:

Find the cabin.

Day Seven:

Open the basement door.

Elias dropped the photographs.

“No.”

His phone rang.

3:17.

He answered.

This time, he didn't speak.

The voice on the other end whispered:

“You're ahead of schedule.”

Elias looked at the clock.

3:17.

But it wasn't nighttime.

It was 3:17 in the afternoon.

He looked at the dashboard.

The date was wrong.

September 28.

Then September 27.

Then September 26.

The date began changing rapidly.

His phone started displaying photographs.

Hundreds.

Thousands.

All of him.

Sleeping.

Eating.

Driving.

Walking.

Dying.

Again.

Again.

Again.

Different rooms.

Different years.

Different versions of himself.

In one photograph, he was an old man.

In another, a child.

In another, he was standing in a room he had never seen.

And behind him—

something was smiling.

---

Elias drove north.

He didn't know why.

He knew where he was going.

The forest appeared exactly as it had in the photographs.

At its center stood a small wooden cabin.

He stopped outside.

The door was already open.

Inside was a table.

On the table sat seven photographs.

He picked up the first.

It showed him entering the cabin.

He picked up the second.

It showed him walking downstairs.

The third showed him opening a metal door.

The fourth showed him looking inside.

The fifth showed him screaming.

The sixth showed him falling.

The seventh showed him lying dead.

Elias put them down.

“No.”

For the first time, the phone didn't ring.

Instead, someone behind him spoke.

“You finally understand.”

Elias slowly turned.

A man stood in the doorway.

Same height.

Same face.

Same eyes.

Same scar above the eyebrow.

Elias couldn't breathe.

The man smiled.

“I've been trying to warn you.”

“Who are you?”

The other Elias looked tired.

“I’m you.”

“That isn't possible.”

“I know.”

“Then what is this?”

The other Elias looked toward the basement.

“A prison.”

“For who?”

The man looked directly into his eyes.

“For us.”

---

Elias backed away.

“How many?”

The other man didn't answer.

“How many versions of me?”

The man finally whispered:

“Enough to fill the basement.”

The floorboards creaked.

Something moved underneath them.

Then another sound.

Then another.

Hundreds of voices.

All whispering the same sentence.

“Let me out.”

Elias stared at the basement door.

The other Elias walked toward it.

“Don't.”

He stopped.

“Why?”

“Because every version of us eventually opens it.”

“And?”

The man looked at him.

“And every version of us becomes the one making the phone calls.”

Silence.

Elias understood.

The caller wasn't warning him.

The caller wasn't predicting his death.

The caller was guiding him toward it.

Because someone had to become the next caller.

The other Elias stepped aside.

The basement door waited.

Elias stared at it for a long time.

Then he laughed.

Quietly.

The other Elias frowned.

“What?”

Elias looked at him.

“You made one mistake.”

“What?”

“I don't have to open it.”

The other Elias smiled.

“You already did.”

Elias froze.

He looked down.

His hand was on the basement handle.

He didn't remember touching it.

The door opened.

Darkness waited beneath.

And from somewhere deep below came the sound of a phone ringing.

3:17.

Elias slowly descended the stairs.

The other Elias watched him disappear.

Then he closed the door.

For the first time in years, he smiled.

His phone rang.

He answered.

On the other end was Elias.

Terrified.

Breathing heavily.

“Who are you?”

The other Elias closed his eyes.

And whispered the words he had heard thousands of times before:

“You have six days left.”

Then he hung up.

And waited.

Because tomorrow, he would receive another call.

From another version of himself.

And someday—

one of them would finally understand.

There was never a monster in the basement.

The monster was the person who opened the door.

THE LAST CIVILIZATION BEFORE GOD

There was a universe before ours.

And it did not end with an explosion.

It ended with a scream.

---

PROLOGUE — BEFORE THERE WAS LIGHT

Before stars existed, there was a world.

Not Earth.

Not a planet.

Not even a universe.

It existed in a place where the words inside and outside had no meaning.

The people who lived there called their home Erebus.

They had oceans without shores.

Mountains that grew downward.

Forests whose trees remembered things that had never happened.

Their sky was black—not because there was no light, but because light itself had not yet learned how to exist.

And the people of Erebus were not primitive.

They had conquered everything.

They had learned to control matter.

They had learned to manufacture living organisms.

They could create artificial weather inside cities.

They could build suns.

They could erase diseases before they appeared.

They could preserve a human mind after the body had died.

They could travel between dimensions.

Eventually, they discovered something even greater.

They discovered that their entire reality was alive.

At first, they celebrated.

Then they became afraid.

Because their universe was not merely alive.

It was watching them.

---

I — THE PEOPLE WHO HAD NOTHING LEFT TO FEAR

The civilization called itself the Vael.

There were approximately twelve trillion of them.

They had no kings.

No governments.

No wars.

No poverty.

No hunger.

No natural death.

Every Vael received a body engineered specifically for them.

Some were made for oceans.

Some for the upper atmosphere.

Some for the deep planetary crust.

Some lived as clouds of biological particles.

Some had abandoned physical bodies entirely and existed as thinking structures of light and mathematics.

Their civilization had lasted for approximately nine hundred billion years.

They had conquered galaxies that did not resemble galaxies.

They had crossed dimensions that human mathematics could never describe.

They had encountered civilizations older than their own.

And destroyed them.

Not because they were cruel.

Because they were curious.

The Vael believed that everything existed to be understood.

And anything that could not be understood...

was eventually destroyed.

Their greatest scientists were called The Anatomists.

They studied reality itself.

They dissected stars.

They opened black holes.

They examined the fundamental structure of time.

They discovered that time was not a dimension.

It was an organism.

They discovered that gravity was not a force.

It was a form of memory.

They discovered that matter was not dead.

Every particle contained a microscopic instinct to remain itself.

Everything was alive.

Everything was thinking.

Everything was afraid.

The Vael simply did not care.

Until they found the door.

---

II — THE DOOR

It appeared beneath their oldest city.

There was no excavation.

No earthquake.

No warning.

One morning, the ground opened.

Inside was a staircase.

It descended infinitely.

The Vael sent machines first.

The machines returned six minutes later.

Every machine had been turned inside out.

Not physically.

Conceptually.

Their cameras were facing themselves.

Their memories contained footage of their own destruction.

Their artificial minds had developed a new emotion.

Fear.

Then they began whispering one sentence:

"Do not wake it."

The scientists laughed.

They sent people.

The first expedition contained 400 soldiers.

They descended.

Three hours later, one returned.

Her name was Serah.

She had been alive for seventeen thousand years.

She had never cried.

She was considered psychologically incapable of fear.

When she returned, she had no eyes.

The doctors asked what happened.

She answered:

«"It looked at me."»

"What looked at you?"

Serah remained silent.

Then she whispered:

«"Everything."»

---

III — THE THING BELOW

The Vael did not stop.

They never stopped.

They sent their greatest scientists into the staircase.

They reached a chamber.

Inside the chamber was a sphere.

Perfectly black.

Perfectly smooth.

It had no mass.

No temperature.

No radiation.

No detectable energy.

Yet every instrument screamed when pointed at it.

The scientists studied it for 73 years.

Then they discovered something.

The sphere was not an object.

It was an egg.

And something inside was dreaming.

The Vael attempted to communicate.

They transmitted mathematics.

The sphere responded.

They transmitted language.

It responded.

They transmitted memories.

It responded.

Eventually they transmitted the collective history of their civilization.

The sphere remained silent.

Then it sent one message.

Not in language.

Not in mathematics.

It sent a memory.

A memory of the Vael.

But the memory was from the future.

They watched themselves running.

Millions of them.

Billions.

Trillions.

Running through endless darkness.

Screaming.

Begging.

Trying to escape something behind them.

Then the message ended.

The scientists argued.

Some wanted to destroy the sphere.

Others wanted to open it.

The oldest scientist among them, Aren, asked one question.

"Where did the memory come from?"

Nobody answered.

Then Aren noticed something.

The memory contained something that should not exist.

A person.

Standing among the screaming Vael.

Watching them.

Smiling.

The person was Aren.

---

IV — THE FIRST DEATH

Aren died the next morning.

His body was found inside his laboratory.

There were no wounds.

His organs were intact.

His brain was intact.

His heart was still beating.

But his body had aged approximately six hundred billion years.

He had become dust while remaining alive.

The doctors could not understand it.

Then Aren opened his mouth.

His voice was ancient.

Not weak.

Not dying.

Ancient.

He said:

«"We are not the first."»

Then his heart stopped.

Five seconds later, every clock in the city stopped.

For exactly one minute.

Then all of them started again.

But they were counting backward.

---

V — THE UNIVERSE SPEAKS

The Vael finally understood.

Their universe had been communicating with them for billions of years.

Every earthquake.

Every star.

Every black hole.

Every biological mutation.

Every death.

Every coincidence.

Everything had been language.

They had simply been too arrogant to listen.

The universe was not their home.

It was their prison.

And the staircase was not a door leading outward.

It was a door leading inward.

Into the mind of the universe.

The Vael had spent billions of years believing they had conquered reality.

They had misunderstood.

Reality had allowed them to conquer it.

Because reality wanted them to reach the center.

---

VI — THE LAST WAR

The Vael declared war against existence itself.

They activated weapons capable of destroying dimensions.

They opened artificial black holes.

They collapsed entire realities.

They attempted to kill the universe before the universe could kill them.

For seven thousand years, they fought.

Reality did not fight back.

That frightened them more than anything.

The universe simply watched.

Then one day...

the stars began disappearing.

Not exploding.

Not dying.

Disappearing.

One.

Then another.

Then millions.

Then billions.

The darkness spread.

The Vael looked toward the edge of existence.

Something enormous was approaching.

It was impossible to see its shape.

Because there was nothing outside it.

It was not moving through space.

Space was moving around it.

Then every living Vael heard the same voice.

Inside their minds.

A voice older than matter.

Older than time.

Older than death.

It said:

«"You wanted to know what was beyond everything."»

The Vael stood frozen.

«"Now you will know."»

---

VII — THE END

The universe began collapsing.

Not inward.

Backward.

Mountains became dust.

Dust became atoms.

Atoms became energy.

Energy became something smaller.

Time began running backward.

People remembered their deaths before remembering their births.

Children remembered being ancient.

Ancient beings remembered being unborn.

Entire civilizations disappeared from history.

Then from memory.

Then from possibility.

The Vael screamed.

They built shelters.

They created artificial universes.

They escaped into dimensions where time moved differently.

Nothing worked.

The universe followed them.

One by one, their artificial universes died.

One by one, their dimensions closed.

The Vael had conquered everything.

And now there was nowhere left to run.

---

VIII — SERAH

Serah was the last surviving Vael.

She returned to the staircase.

She descended alone.

At the bottom, she found the black sphere.

It was no longer closed.

Something was standing inside it.

A human-shaped figure.

Tall.

Thin.

Featureless.

Serah looked at it.

For the first time in her seventeen thousand years of life...

she was afraid.

The creature spoke.

Its voice sounded like every dead civilization speaking simultaneously.

«"Do you know why your universe is ending?"»

Serah said nothing.

The creature continued.

«"Because it was never a universe."»

The walls began shaking.

Serah looked upward.

There was no ceiling anymore.

Only darkness.

«"It was a thought."»

Serah finally understood.

Their entire reality...

their billions of years...

their civilizations...

their wars...

their loves...

their memories...

their discoveries...

everything...

had existed inside something else's mind.

The creature looked at her.

«"And the mind is waking."»

---

IX — THE BIG BANG

Serah screamed.

Not because she was dying.

Because she suddenly understood what came next.

The creature raised one hand.

The entire previous reality collapsed into a single point.

Everything became infinitely dense.

Everything became infinitely hot.

Time disappeared.

Space disappeared.

Matter disappeared.

Memory disappeared.

Then—

nothing.

For the first time in nine hundred billion years...

there was silence.

And then...

a tiny spark.

A flash.

A beginning.

The universe exploded outward.

Stars formed.

Galaxies formed.

Planets formed.

Oceans formed.

Life appeared.

Millions of years passed.

Then billions.

Eventually...

on one small blue planet...

a human being looked toward the night sky.

He wondered why the universe existed.

He wondered whether anyone had lived before him.

He wondered whether something was watching.

He laughed at himself.

Then he noticed something strange.

One star in the sky had disappeared.

Then another.

Then another.

He stared upward.

And somewhere beyond human perception...

something opened its eyes.

---

EPILOGUE — THE MESSAGE

In the deepest layer of space, beneath every known physical law, something remained.

A fragment.

A single memory from the civilization before the Big Bang.

It had survived the birth of the universe.

Scientists would eventually discover it.

They would call it an anomaly.

They would spend centuries trying to understand it.

They would finally decode it.

It contained only one sentence.

Not a warning.

Not a prayer.

Not a prophecy.

A confession.

"We thought we were the first ones to discover the universe."

Beneath it was another sentence.

Written in a language humanity had not yet invented.

But somehow...

every human who saw it understood.

"You are not the first ones either."

ცხოვრება ტანჯვით არის სავსე.
-
Same process — images work exactly like any other file in git history.

**For a single photo:**
```bash
git filter-repo --path photo.jpg --invert-paths
```
(swap `photo.jpg` for the actual filename)

**For a whole folder of images** (e.g. `images/` or `assets/`):
```bash
git filter-repo --path images/ --invert-paths
```

**With BFG** (often faster for binary files like images):
```bash
bfg --delete-files photo.jpg
# or for a folder:
bfg --delete-folders images
git reflog expire --expire=now --all
git gc --prune=now --aggressive
git push --force
```

**One thing specific to photos**: BFG has a handy option to strip *all* large/binary files above a size threshold, useful if you just want to slim down repo history in general:
```bash
bfg --strip-blobs-bigger-than 1M
```

Same caveats as before — force-push required, old clones need to re-clone, and if the photo was ever public, assume it could've been cached/scraped elsewhere even after removal from GitHub.

Is this for cleaning up an old photo from one of your profile-site repos?
-
Not fully automatic on its own — GitHub doesn't have a "purge history" button — but here's what comes close:

**Closest to automatic:**

1. **GitHub's own tool**: `git filter-repo` (the official recommended tool) — you run one command and it strips the file from all commits automatically:
```bash
git filter-repo --path text.txt --invert-paths
```
This rewrites the entire history in one shot, no manual commit-by-commit editing.

2. **BFG Repo-Cleaner** — even simpler, purpose-built for this:
```bash
bfg --delete-files text.txt
git reflog expire --expire=now --all
git gc --prune=now --aggressive
git push --force
```
Also one-shot automatic.

**Still requires you to:**
- Run the command once
- Force-push after
- Anyone else who cloned the repo needs to re-clone (their old history won't sync)

**True zero-effort option**: Delete the whole repo and re-create it fresh with just the current files (no history at all). That's the only way to remove history without running any cleanup command yourself.

Want me to walk you through the `filter-repo` or BFG steps for your specific repo?
-
If you upload a file with the same name (`text.txt`) to the same folder in a GitHub repo:

- **Via GitHub web UI**: it detects the existing file and asks you to confirm — it will **replace/overwrite** the old content with the new one in a new commit.
- **Via git push**: your local file just overwrites the tracked file, and pushing creates a commit with the diff.
- **Old content isn't lost**: it stays accessible in the commit history — you can always view or revert to the previous version.
- Your site's live version updates immediately (or after Pages rebuilds, if you're using GitHub Pages) to reflect the new file content.

So if you're uploading an "upgraded" `text.txt` with more/different posts, it'll just replace the old one — as long as your parser script still expects the same format (e.g. split by `-` lines), it'll pick up the new content automatically without needing code changes.

If your post-feed parsing logic depends on a specific structure, want me to check what it expects before you upload the new file?
